The lid inquest, held by the local Coroner during the period covered by the report m the Watch Com- mittee included burning fatalities, and a consideration Cl snch leads naturally to the topic demoted by the heed line. Mr. Frank Ormerod, a fellow-journalist whom I brew when in Rochdale, has written in the " Pall Mall Magazine " on both the woollen and the cotton industry, and his article on the latter included a paragraph headed A word for English flenneleve.- As he said therein, there is cothing that has no largely brought discredit on cotton during the last few years as the coroner'e condemnation of the " deadly flannelette," an article which, as everybody knows, is made entirely of rotten, "raised " on one or both sidee after manufacture to gins it a feeling of warmth and comfort. But (my friend went on) there is nothing, also, that has been so wrong• headeilly aluseo, for, to Tut the thing is a nutshell. good flannelette is as safe a material as almost any- thing we wear, fiord latinelette ouch as is produced by reputable Englidt firfne is made from pure cotton. in Cl‘ wily woven, and has very little " nap." but the article which Iso often arouses indignation is more often than not it German or other foreign product made from cotton waists, woven as coarsely as sacking and raised into a mass of fluff. Tee coroner sees 110 difference apparently, 'but it is there, nevertheless,' and the difference is accentuated by about sixpence a yard in price. And an, in-tend cf advising parents totakeordinatycare with their children, to put guards round their tires, and to buy the reliable article, coroners neck to prejudice the public mind against a good. cheap, and u).eful tandem n product that ha, added to the happiness and comfort of thousands. In a cotton mantifacturing district in which I worked . for many years, an enterprising firm patented: a pr.) cess for rendern.g flannelette non- inflammable and poor people will do well to note this. Mr. thnerod, however, cares not for ouch devices ; in fact, lie is rather severe on rnealanres of this character. I do not myself believe that his criticism applies to the excellent invention of the firm with whom I am acquainted, and for whom I have nr thing but commendation. Besides, if my experience goes for anything, be is wrong in suggesting that coroners do not warn parents to use fire-guards. SOME FUNNY ADVERTISEMENTS. Did you ever collect funny advertisements? I don't mean the ones that are meant to be funny ; I mean those that are serious hut , are worded in a curious way. Those who I look out for humour will find it everywhere and especially in advertisements. Perhaps you will begin to collect them and paste or copy in a scrap book. If so, I will give a few to begin with. This is what we call unconscious humour, and every one is copied from a genuine advertisement. Annual sale now on. Dori't go elsewhere to be cheated—come in here.

A lady wants to sell her piano, as she is going away, in a strong iron frame. Wanted—Experienced nurse for bottled baby.

Furnished apartments suitable for gentle- men with folding doors. Two sisters want washing. Wanted—A room by two gentlemen about thirty feet long and twenty feet broad. Lost—A collie dog by a man on Saturday answering to Jim with a brass collar round his neck and a muzzle.

Wanted, by a respectable girl, her passage to New York; willing to take care of children and a good sailor. Wanted—A boy for butchering. Respectable widow wants washing on Tuesdays. For sale—A pianoforte, the property of a musician withicarved legs. Mr. Brown, furrier, begs to announce that he will make up gowns, capes, etc., for ladies out of their own skins. A boy who can open oysters with reference. Bulldog for sale; will eat anything ; very fond of children.

Wanted—An organist and a boy to blow the same.

Wanted—a boy to be partly outside and partly behind the counter. THIS WEEKS STORY. A HUMAN QUEEN. ---• . . _

I When a girl, Wilhel-mina of Holland. possessed a family of dolls, of whom she remained particularly fond long after her skirts had been let down. She was a motherly little person, and could not bear to put away her pets. One was the Queen of Doll-land, and was dressed in miniature robes of royalty. It had also a tiny throne, and other dolls appointed as attendants. One day, after coming home very tired from the celebration of her own birthday. Wilhelmina got out her queen doll and made her bow and bend till her veil and crown were terribly awry. Now," said the little girl, pettishly, " how do you like being a queen? Doesn't your back ache, and don't you feel horrid ?"

LIFTING CATS ANI; mours.

It its a mistaken idea that the proper may to lift a full grown cst is by the nape of its neck without supporting the lower part of its body with the other hand. It is true that the mother cat carries young kittens by grasping in her mouth the loose skin at the back of her offspring's neck, but a tiny kitten is a very different matter from a large cat, and, indeed. the only may to lift a kitten without squeezing or hurting its soft little body is to lift it by its neck. But after it has grown larger its own weight is too great to be supported by such a bit of skin and fur Os is grasped by the hand, and many a cat suffers ! p,feet tortures by being held in this manner, and is quite helpless to run or struggle, as in such a position certain Cl its muscles canuct be controlled, and it is I absolutely at the mercy of its unfeeling tormentor. The rams rule should be observed in libing rabbits by their ears. They should alwaya be partially supported by the free hind and not allowed to dangle with their whole weight straining from their large but necessarily delicate ears.

FIRST ANNUAL MEETING.

Organised Charity versus Casual Giving

Although it has only been in existence a little over a year, the Warrington Citizens Guild of Help ham done good work amongst the poor and needy in the town, and is rapidly growing to be one of Warring- ton's most useful charitable Institutions. JOTTINGS BY AN OLD JOURNALIST.

THE PASSWORD? The allusion to Sir Thomas Liston in the coon. of Mr. G.K.Chenterton.a lecture at Warrington reminded me of a little incident which came under my notice while under canvas As press correspondent during the annual training of a battalion of Lancashire Territorials, I had hem n accemmorlated with:, tent which happened to Inc at the end of the line nearest the main entrance to the encampment. One night, being thor uglily tired out. I had turned in earlier than usual, but, owing to the returning stream .of fellows on leave. and the constant repetition of the sentries' challenge was unable to sleep. The hoardings of the town front which our citizen soldiers had come hail been freely placarded with moments- Cons of a prune porker bearira the legend " Lipton's leading article." It was impossible for anyone remaining about the district to get out of the Way of this flaming advertisement. and, as the sequel proved, it Was destined to haunt me even in my camp bed- stead down by the sea. As on the night in question I beguiled ini wakeful nioments by listening to the remarks of the late arrivals, the sentry's "Who comes there?" rang out once more and the thin voice of a juvenile bugler rem inded " Lipton's leading article.' THE SENTRY'S TURN TO SCOPE. The youngster just referred to raised a laugh at the expense of the sentry, but the latter has many a good story to his credit. Not long ago I read in the is Green How-tads' Gasette " bonn a bite Governor of Gibralter,who WaS alto a strict disciplinarian, wan in the habit of making surprise visits to the sentries at night. time he came °omen a man asleep at bin poet. The Governor lifted the sentry's rifle, and was quietly walking away with it when the sleeper awakened. The poor fellow was stunned for a moment, lint I divining the Governor's intention, he followed cauti- ously behind. Arrived at the smanlroom. the guilty sentry's rifle wag placed amongst the others on the rack *outside. The Governor then turned for a moment to call the ftergeant of the guard, but iv that moment the sentry quietly but quickly seised his rifle, and was promptly back at his post. The sergeant, as instructed. cosmted the rifles, and reported " All correct. sir." "Nonsense ! Count again," said the triumphant Governor. " Quite' correct, sir," again replied the sergeant, after a careful inspection of the rack. "There must lie a mistake somewhere," exclaimed the Governor, "for I Inane Met this moment placed in the rack it ride belong- ing to the sentry at No. —tent. Come with me." " Halt ! Who goes there ?" came loud and clear from the now very wide-awake sentry. as Governor and sergeant approtiched his post.. The Governor was petrified. Ile glared into the innocent-looking face of the sentry before him, muttered something that flavoured of britnstone, and, turning on his heel, vanished. Then the sentry winked, and the sergeant smiled ! THE CONSCRIPTIONISTS. I have come other personal reeollections of military life under mums for! hare hail varied experience, with the old volunteers ; but I must hasten along, with a peening obnervation on the nubject dealt with oar last week's leader under the heading which have quoted. I think it wait lord Milner robs con- gested that there is a great deal more in military trebling than in the training of the body,his contention being than it is a model training, which is invaluable lath from the poiet of view ef the workman and from the point of view of the citizen. I commend this to onr young men who are sort of esquetting with the territorial scheme. Unfertunately there are infinen-i tial persone who persistently decry the re-organised force and declare the whole thing to be a failure. I venture respectfully to urge that little value need be attached to the opinions of the obviously Initiated. and especially to thise who are strongly prejudiced in favour of a system of conscr,ption. To be sure the Territorial Force is not at its full strength. That's a pity, but of couree it is not the fault of the office's and men, who are doing their best tinnier heavy handicap ; the blame and the shame are with thole, eligible but indifferent young fellows who continue to turn a deaf ear to the voice of the recruiting charmer, cbartn he never so wisely. Moreover if the form is not adequately equipped, it is for the War Office that called it to existence to see to Met, Inonoord, as someone has well raid, this is not a time for panic-stricken measures. by which a superstructure not yet complete must he razed to the ground, the foundation, lulled out. and I.uilding started anew. All the evidence of the last Territorial Camp goes to show that the men are of the right quality. They cannot be licked into shape without time mid patience. But they are the raw material nut of which a capital reserve force can he merle, and it would be a grievmrs waste of time and energy to pull down, at the bidding , of a few pessimists, that which has already been accomplinheil. Let the War Office give the right kind of eneouragement to officers and flier], and the Territorial Force will prove its worth. At all events Ike country will certainly not have conscription. PRACTICAL POULTRY FARMING.

(By Redcap.)

FEBIWARY NOTES.

It is time now to set eggs from the heavy breeds. March hatched bird, make the beet winter layers an a rule, so that no time should be lost in getting down a few egg... If you intend uning a broody hen. see that she is set in a very sheltered corner. The weather at present in very cold and many risks attend even February hatching. By the time (three weeks hence) the chickens make their appearance we hope for more congenial weedier. no take great care of the broody hen during the process of incubation ; then you may rxpect a successful batch of chicks to rear. Whether you are purchasing eggs or sitting from your own stock. be careful in the selection. Choose only the bent shaped and strongest shelled ones for the purpose name!. It is no use risking thin shelled eggs, neither in it profitable to use either were large or very small eggs. Some people imagine that if they set large eggs they will get large chicks. Nature herself teaches otherwise ; for in it not a fact that the smallest lowls lays (nearly always) the biggest eggs. In point of fact you trill hear sic out in that ; The Andalunian beats the Orpington junt uneasily as the Miners beats the Cochin for size of eggs. So do not he misguided by size; always set the best shaped and medium sized ones. few notes on Ihow to manage

71, 1111.,4,1, }fr.

may not be out of place at this time. First of all be sure that she "means bitsinese." Nothing is more annoying than having a valuable sitting of eggs spoiled by the hen not Sitting her time out. It is not always convenient to replace her in the space of half- an-hour. Be sure then that she is thoroughly broody. and never risk a broody pullet if an old hen can be I procured. Observing these essentials you may pro- ceed. Make the rest in the quietest corner possib'e. If on the ground floor so much the better, providing . always that it is not too damp. If the nest has to be made on a boarded flour then place in it first of all, a good thick std. After making firm and even, place in it also a good thickness soft hay sad well sprinkle the whole with Keating's insect powder. Having thus far proceeded you at, introduce the liars' to her new ijaartcr,. ho this at night and in doing an, place a few " pot" eggs under the hen. If the following morning she seems quite content and the false eggs warm, then you may risk the gennine articles. These also should be placed tinder at night and when she is still on the nest. These simple pre- cautions are very necessary to avoid breakages. W ten a full sitting of eggs are placed in the nest and the lien allowed to go un of lice MVP free trill there is a danger that she may clumsily smash one or two.

Cr. list. A sitting hen should never be fed oftener than once a day. This should be given about nine or ten o'clock in the forenoon, when the rest of the stock are sway foraging and all is quiet. It will be quite obvious that the daily meal should consist of a sub- stantial and good finality grain. Indian corn Cr maize is the very best to give, for it not only takes a long time to digest and waste in the system, hut it also sepplies that necessary amount of int.. rnal fat no essential to supply the fal which gives beat to the lien's body. This feeding, you are aware. is to be avoided with !eying stock, but should be .ught after in the 1118132g..100t of the sitting hen. Very well, then. throw down a good feed of corn, place a vessel of clean drinking water close bv.and in a small box put a handful of sharp flint grit, bunt forget the dust-bath. This should he composed of a shallow box roll of tire sand and fine ashes, to which has been added a handful of powdered sulpher. The later will destroy any existing vermin. Having thus far made preparations you may then releitie the hen. If not inclined to leave the nest after a fair trial you should gently lift her flora the nest and then prevent her getting hack egain until she has had a good meal. When you are tiatistied that she has thus partaken, let her go back again but to not chive her on utilises forced to do au. If the hen is thoroughly broody and ' keen on • you will have tin difficulty in this respect.

THE ECo,,

It is quite safe to test the fertility of the eggs after a week has elapsed. It is useless for the hen to cover unfertile eggs; the heat of her body can more profitably be given to the fertile ones. There- fore • few hints on egg-Wetting may be glean. This may be done by holding each egg in front of an oblong hole (the size of an egg) winch hue been cut out of a piece of carollaArd, pieces candle at the bock of the opening an that the contents rf the rag may be seen. If the eggs appear clear against the light. these may be discarded an unfert;le. If on the other hand. dark patch appears, then you can he sure that such I contain a chick. Do not threw the deer eggs away as usele-a, for they are useful when hard-billed as !chicken food ; indeed, some people use them for i cooking purposes.

........ If you are purchasing eggs from a dlstance do not ' net 'bent straight away ; they ..bould be allowed to settle for a day before piecing under the ben. It la a good plan when possible to net two hens at the came time, so that if you only get moderate hatches, all could be given over to the care r f one hen ; thus saving extra trouble and attention. The period of incubation is teensy-one de; s. Da not be surprised if some of the chicks appear snap or two tanner or even a dcv Sr two later than this. Much derends opt° the freshness of the ego' when set. Weather conditions aso effect the general period at tim.s. When the eggs begin to chip do not ta,iper mitt, them Let nature have Lor curiae. tin chicken is not strong enough to break through,the chances are that it would alwaye las weak eves if a-siated by breaking the shell. Chickens require no food for the first twenty-four hours of their existence, therefore I would ask you to rliacard all "old woman methods" of giving them a drink, it 14 cruel to do so. During the later stage of incubation they absorb the con- tents of the yolk-bag and this is sufficient to nourish tnem for the time stared. If the hen is of an uneasy dispoeition it might be a wise plan to remove each chick to sure being tranipled on. 11.,, place them in a flannel-lined basket, keep them warm but not too hot. having made sure that all have hatched out, cleor the neat of broken shells and gently return the chicks to the hen. Then leave her to quietly settle flown. In the noontime you will have prepared a coop to receive the new &mile and when yon are sure that the hen has nettled, transfer them to the coop. They will have more freclont wad Into ratli of berng trampled on.

. .... As I have elready pointed out the chicks need no f orsl for the first2l hour.. Warrington j lurney to the confines of the Union to plly Coventry at the Midland City, and while they should win this engagement. the result is not the certainty it ought to be considering the respective records and calibre of the teams. Coventry are rather better than their showing in the League suggests, and Warrington, st present, are rather worse on the same standard. Coventry have won 3, and lost 17 match PR, while Warrington have won 11 and lost 8, five being drawn.

The Wirepullers' executive is in a quandary owing to injuries to platers, and the team will sot be finally eelectetl till the night before the match. Readers of this column most refer to the back page of the paper for the selected thirteen. At the time of writing Brooks, Creevey, and Fish are all doubtful starters, and it seems practically certain that the back division will he weak in attacking pwver. It was particularly unfortunate that Brooks should strain a muscle in the thigh last Saturday, for he had been playing in a form which promised excellently fir the cup tournament. Creevey and Fish are both on the lmrder line of fitness and it tvill be a last-minute decision which determines whether either or neither shall play.

There is no doubt that a lot of Warrington's respon- sibility will rest on the forwards. The latter have not been seen at their hest for some time, but if etery man does his duty to-day there should not be much doubt about getting possession and possession of the ball is cup-tie strugules is more than half the battle. The Warrington six are big enough and strong enough to hold their own anywhere and against the Coventry pack they should demonstrste their powers in unmistakable fashion, or the match will be lost.

Coventry are one of those teams which seem good on the attack and weak on the defence. Warring- toad qualities are more of the contrary kind. They do not score 01 ten, but if they get a lead they should keep it. The Coventry men have twom.eful halves in Yewlett and Beynon the ex-Oldham pair, and a couple of fast threequarters in Woodburn and Harrison. One thing the team will certainly lack and that is Cup.tie experience. This .hould give Warrington the pull when the struggle becomes keenest.

On their first visit to coventry early in September. Warringom only succeeded in making a draw of ten points each. The team which made the journey teas not one that inspired much confidence. for the wings were Clare and Landor, both of inborn gave a poor display, and missed chances of scoring which should have WOO the match with ease. The forwards were aiso weak in comparison with the six available to-day, :Ind probably Poison and Chesters are the only two who will make a return visit to-day. Shugara, Thomas, Johnston and Skelhorn were absentees on the first occasion.

A Coventry correspondent writes: The supporters of the Coventry Northern Union Club are looking forward to the ViAlt of Warrington. In the previous round of the cop Coventry met Merthyr Tydvil at Coventry, and easily overcame the Welsh side by 18 points to three.

The club have been going through a special course of training, and will he sound and fit for a hard game. Life and Works."

The Warringt in Pink:nitride Society is to be con- gratulated upon having arranged for the visit to Warrington of Mr. Alfred Tennyson Dickens, eldest surviving son of the late Chide, Dickens, who will lecture at the Parr Hall oa Tuesday. February 2ith. on "My father's life and works." Mr. Dickens was bore at Devonshire House, opp-site Marylebone Church, London, on the 2.Ath day of October. Irtls. In company with his three brothers (Frank. Sydney, and Harry) he was sent to irhool, first at Hautville, Hue de L,ratoire, fluilogne, remaining there five years, afterwards at Brackenbury's 11ilitary School, Wimbledon, where a cadetship in the East India . Company's service was procured for him which lapsed lin cmisequence of the East India Service being amalgamated widr the Imperial Service. Whilst at Wimbledon his father wae writing "Great Expectations," and Mr. Dickens well remember.: the book being discuseeri among the family. 1m1r,3 found Mr. Dickens in t,,e city with T. A. t hibb and Co., who dealt in tea, silk, &c. He served there for two years and then decided logo silt to Australia, at his father's desire. His brother Ted, the youngest of the family, joined him two years later, and Charles Dickens wrote the celebrated letter to hint on leaving. Ia IS7'd Mr. Dickens was manager of a station at Forbes on the Lachlan, where he had the pleasure of welcoming Anthony Trollope, whose son Fred had a station near. Mr. Dickens frequently bad letters from his father, the last one in " Lettere of Charles Dickens " is to him. For the last twenty years Mr. Dickens has been lecturing through Australia on "My lather's Life and Works" with enormOus success.

THE EXAMINER.

SATURDAY. FEBRUARY 19. 1911

The minds of the THE HOME RULE most experienced DEBATE. members of the House of Commons must have been carried back on Wednesday in a tumult of recollections. The Opposition raised the Home Rule question on all amendment to the Address. There must have sprung to many men's eyes the picture of Mr. GLADSTONE, with ,his eagle face, his piercing and searching eyes, his deep, rich voice, capable of all modulations, moving the second reading of the first Home Rule Bill. Since that time "the seas have rolled between." How much has been changed • in the interval in respect of the Home Rule question? Mr. WINSTON CHURCHILL gives us the best answer. Home Rule is now discussed in a different atmo- sphere, and even in a new climate. Most of the old hostile arguments are not only worn thin, but are disused. Ways of putting things that roused passion in former days are listened to with a certain cold indifference. Fires that burned fiercely heretofore have died down into ashes that are now scarcely warm. "Where,' asked Othello, "is the supernal spark that will that light re-ilumine ? " There can be no more beating up the old anti-Home Rule feeling to incan- descence. The time for mere windy and uncontrolled rhetoric has gone by, even if the disposition to consider the matter calmly has not yet arrived. The Opposition amendmert was on lines that have long been familiar. The Opposition wants the details of the Home Rule Bill to play with. That is the long and short of it. On Wednesday Mr. lAN MALCOLM and Lord HUGH CECIL attempted with much ingenuity to make it appear that Ministers are not acquainted with their own intentions, and that the Irish party has no reason to be satis- fied with any declarations that have been made, On th 3 last point, of course, Mr. JOHN REDMOND is a better authority than Lord HUGH CECIL, and he said that the Nationalist party is in no way distressed or confused about the intentions of the Govern- ment. It understands what Mr. ASQUITH said at the Albert Hall, and it believes alike in his ability and his sincerity. Great legis- lative measures are, of course, not produced, or at any rate are not produced long, in advance of their introduction to the House of Commons. The Prime Minister can have left no doubt in the House as to the absolute clearness of his mind on the course that is to be taken, and even Mr. O'BRIEN announced that he does not doubt Mr. ASQUITH'S sincerity. All that he does doubt is the power of the Ministry to pass the Bill. Surely this is a motive, not for speeches that are in the nature of obstacles, but for deter- mining to "wait and see." It was a brilliantly persuasive and high- minded speech which came from Mr. WINSTON CHURCHILL He reminded the House that, since the two former Home Rule Bills were before Parliament, questions have been settled that have been harassing British Governments for generations past. There has been a long procession of Irish remedial measures. Even within the last five year!,

prodigious things have happened. "South Africa has happened," said Mr. CHURCHILL, referring to the great Act of Union which has taken place on what must no longer by too broad a generalisation be described as the" Dark Continent." Home Rule no longer presents itself in the guise of the early 'Eighties.' The situation is illumined by the South African solution of a similar, and as it seemed, a more difficult problem. Is the Irish settlement to be postponed, Mr. CHURCHILL asked, by further conflicts between Liberal and Tory' Is nothing to be done by Parliament as a whole? If only principle could be placed before passion and rancour, Home Rule, he maintained with great elositence and force, should be the gift, not of a party only, but of the nation. How- ever, Mr. BALFOUR had declined to join in the South African settlement, and he had been supported by his party. That was a fact of which history would make disapprov- ing note. 'The party opposite will have another chance," said the HOME SECRETARY, in the closing sentences of a speech which was a high appeal for patriotic conduct. "Don't" he added, "choose wrongly again."

We are definitely MR. ROBERT informed that Mr. PIERPOINT. Robert Pierpoint, the

. - late-Conservative mem- ber for Warrington, has purchased a house in Cleveland-square, Hyde Park, London, and will, ere long, forsake St. Austin's for the metropolis. The news will, we are sure, be learned with regret by a great majority of the townspeople of Warrington whatever their political shade or colour. Mr. Pierpoint was something more than the representative of a party. During his thirty years active association with the progress of the community—nearly 14 of which were spent as Parliamentary representative—Mr. Pierpoint earned ths warm regard of all Warringtonians, and the coming severance is one to be deplored. One does not need to recapitulate the chain of cir- cumstances which have led to Mr. Pierpoint taking the step he has done. His treatment by the Conservative Party has become a. bye-word, and the suggestion at Monday's meeting of the Conservative Association that Mr. Pierpoint should be made a Free- man of the Borough comes with little grace from such a source. The party should have done Mr Pierpoint honour while he was in its midst, and manifested in a more tangible and personal form the regard for him which is so fulsomely expressed at such a late hour.

The Guild of Help is CHARITY IN gradually findings firm WARRINGTON. place among Warring- ton Institutions,though at the present moment it is so far from being self-supporting that a certain amount of pessimism about its future might be excused. The list of subscribers is disappointing, and were it not for some handsome donations the Society would be" in Queer-street." It seems to us that the Guild will be crippled both in its work and its finances unless some modes rivendi is found which will ensure co-operation between the various existing charitable organisations in Warrington and the new corner. It is not unnatural that the older societies feel an objection to being grouped together—departmentalised under one head, so to say—but they should be willing to admit and realise that more efficient and valuable work can be done if they are working shoulder to shoulder instead of competing in public almsgiving. The ideal arrangement would be for all to unite, but if this is too much to expect, then we hope the ladies and gentlemen concerned will give full consideration to the value of at least co-operating with the Guild and putting charitable work on such a footing that it will not be largely wasted through too much diffusion of the common aim.

There is disquieting EDUCATION RATE news for the Warring- RISING, ton ratepayers this week. The Education Committee is once more called upon to take stock of its financial requirements and the " Budget " to be submitted at the monthly meeting on Monday evening flex twill involve an increase in the call on the rates by that authority of nearly 21d. in the L. The total estimated deficiency on the ensuing year's working is £21,756 175., but the committee has built up such a substantial working balance that only £20,500 is to be provided from the Borough rate, as against £lB,OOO for the year just ended. Last year's education rate was is. 41d. in the —a reduction ofd. on the previous twelve months—and as a penny rate produces £l,OBO, the new figure will roughly be Is. VA. The chief cause of the increased deficiency is easily apparent. The abolition of fees in the elementary schools deprives the committee of a source of income which last year was estimated to produce £1,600. Another indirect result of this bold step by the authority is an advance in the amount payable for rent of buildings from £BO to £266 occasioned by the conversion into Council schools of the Cairo-street and Thewlis-street schools which were previ- ously under voluntary management. The salaries payable to teachers in the elementary schools will absorb £31,245 as against £30,755 last year and the possible grant to War- rington Grammar School accounts for an additional I:200. A significant fail is that the Government grant from customs and excise—commonly called " whisky money" —is expected to yield only £630 as compared with £B5O. The steady decline in this source of revenue of late years can only ho attributed to increased sobriety—a pleasing sign at which even the long-suffering rate- payer who has to pay the piper will not be disposed to cavil.

The most dramatic "INCURABLE incident of the debate SLOPPINESS." on the Address arose out of Mr. Austen Chamberlain's attempt to prove that Tariff Reform would have averted this Reciprocity Agreement between Canada and the United States. Mr. Austen Chamberlain quoted what he declared to be an utter- ance of Sir Wilfred Laurier's in the Canadian Parliament. Under Mr. Asquith's searching cross-examination the fabricated testimony collapsed. The passage came from a correspondent's message in the "Standard "—there was not a trace of it in the official report of the speech. It was a mere " Yellow Press " invEntion, the kind of thing by which the Tariff Reform Press has sedulously striven toimislead the public. Of course Mr. Austen Chamberlain has been grievously misled. It was really " hard lines" on him. But he should have known better than trust his own " Yellow Press," and Mr. Asquith crushingly pointed out that this was only a typical case of the "incurable sloppiness" about matters of fact which has characterised the Tariff Reform agitation throughout.

A fter all, the great 'TS WEIGH MORE importance of the HAN ARGUMENT. Agreement lies in some thingbeyond argument. .nts and circumstances weigh down the 4 cogent argumer ts in the scales of 'mon sense. The Agreement shows that most elaborate Preference system could overturned in the twinkling of an

by any foreign country which, cad of bargaining about recipro- simply throws down its tariff 1. Tariff Reformers say that the Agree- it will take the Canadian corn into the tted States which we need. But if the ited States take off their tariff, without bargain at all, what Preference would p Canadian corn for this country? Not not 55., no, nor 10s. But while we have whole corn supply of the world to draw TI, we need not trouble ourselves about ling fiscal arrangements in this country that. The Tariff Reformers want to ire us so dependent on our source of ply that anything which affects it would In starvation for us.

Striking, though no novel, facts in regard the poverty of some livings in the Church England were brought out by the Bishop Gloucester in a sermon on Sunday, at Ay Trinity,Chelsea, on behalf of the Queen ctoria Clergy Fund. He said that in hi. 'n diocese, out of a total of 320 so-called ivings," more than 140 had the net income less than £2OO per annum, and of these er 30 were under £lOO.

* * Mr. Harold Smith, M.P., and Mr. Handel oth, M.P. (Pontefract) both indulged in naiden speeches" in the House on Thurs- y. There was an element of comedy about , e proceedings. The Home Office have issued a statement flouncing the formation of the new central sociation for the aid of discharged prisoners. ie combination is sought under one direct- authority of all the influences, whether force or of friendship, which can be used guide and aid the discharged prisoner. To is end representatives of various philan- ropic societies already concerned with cling prisoners will co-operate with the rthorities through the new agency, which ill take cognisance and control of all dis- larged convicts. The new arrangement ill involve the suspension, except in Isuitable cases, of the "ticket of leave" 'stem of police supervision.

The Rector of Warrington has very •operly referred to the unenviable promin- ute of the borough in regard to drunkenness id suggests that people should not drink ,tween meals, and that the number of tblic-houses should be reduced. Both are ?ry admirable suggestions in their way, but fficult to translate into tangible results. aly education, teaching, and example can tange the habits of the people, and to bring le number of public-houses into the line ith the requirements of the community proper public control of the licensed trade. it received less bolstering from both Church id State the way would be easier.

• "' • Where the Tory party has made its most rious error in recent years has been in not tening to Alderman Bennett. In the rrent issue of the "Sunrise Annual" the Iderman—or a pen very like his—tells us: Chet madness of the House of Lords in rowing out the Budget has brought about e penalty we feared. We personally arned the party leaders of the dangers they ere running; but they listened to the cophants who lied to them instead of to the ,nest friends who dared to give expression unpalatable truths,and,in a sense,although e bitterly lament the situation, their blood upon their heads." Some people in ondon have evidently failed to realise that le real hub of the Universe is Warrington! • •

According to Alderman Bennett, " the isis which now faces the country is the reatest in the history of all civilised ations." Apparently the doom of aristo- stic rule in France and the bloody revolu- on which followed, are to be put in the lade when the free men of England relegate le hereditary house of Peers to its proper ace in the State. If the Alderman is really bsessed by such nightmares he is more ervous than we had imagined.

The February Tariff Reform Monthly otes repeat the out of date and discredited ,atement of Count Komura that because ritain is a Free Trade country, therefore, span could not enter into a Reciprocity reaty with her. This, in spite of the fact iat the Prime Minister, the Foreign Minister id the President of the Board of Trade, as ell as the Japanese Foreign Minister, have ated that the negotiatigns of a Treaty are •ogressing favourably. So far from there lug no room for a Treaty between Great ritain and Japan, the Japenese Minister edicts that such a Treaty will be the first be negotiated. Negotiations with other

countries are not so far advanced as are the negotiations between Japan and Great Britain.

"The Voluntary system," said Mr. Haldane at Bournemouth on Saturday, 'is not only the one we have choeen, but the only one we could choose." That we believe to be the root of the matter, and it is well that the Secretary of State for War should say so thus firmly for the benefit both of his countrymen and of all others with whom "military conversations" may be held. A compulsory service army would strike a fatal blow at the expeditionary force that we must have and is a superfluity for Home Defence, which must always depend on the command of the sea, subject to security against raids. If our Governments were reckless, we might, of course, be committed to a European policy which would require the backing of-a conscript army on the Continental model, and this, we believe, is the real, if unavowed, object of the military school which calls for compulsion. That, for reasons which we have argued at length at other times, we believe to be a fatal deflection of our true policy; and if it were brought about, we should, in our opinion, be weaker and not stronger as a world Power. As Mr. Haldane said on Saturday. the analogies from Continental Powers with land frontiers

to defend are entirely misleading for the Island Empire.—" Westminster Gazette."

Literary and Philosophical Society. The Rev. F. W. Remick, M.A., lectured on " Machiavelli ' to the members of the Literary and Philosophical Society on Monday evening, in the Masonic Hall, Bold-street. Mr. W. Silcock, presided over a good attendance. The Lecturer gave a vivid sketch of Machiavelli's life, but referred more parti- cularly to his book' The l'rince " which wad, he said, perhaps one of the writer's best known works and hest misinterpreted. The Lecturer regarded " The Prince " as a characteristic product of the age and he showed that the real significance of the renaissance was that of a great mental emancipation without any corresponding advance in the sphere of morality. I All the misinterpretations of " The Prince "were due I to the ignoriag of the actual conditions under which it was written It had a definitely circumscribed set of political circumstances in view and it was plainly a mistake to regard it as a manual of tyranny. The writings of Machiavelli, unrivalled as an analysis of contemporary politics, were also an interpretive prophesy of later European history.

Wash Literary Society,

. . The weekly meeting was held last Tuesday evening at the Crostield-street Welsh Chapel. Mr. John Griffiths. presided. A lecture nes delivered in Welsh toy Mr. D. James on Dr. D. fteei LL.B. Litt. D of Cardiganshire, one of the leading Welsh religious preachers of the Llth century. The Lecturer showed that the early surroundings and nurture had their effect on the mental production of the preacher. His pulpit and puritanical sayings were like gems which showed the originality and natural- ness of his thought. He was a great moral force in his local sphere of influence. His wittiness was a marked characteristic of him and the Society on Tuesday had a most enjoyable time in hearing some of the pointed and witty sayings of the old Doctor. The evening which was thoroughly enjoyed was concluded with singing the Welsh National Anthem. Next week the second of the Lantern lectures will be delivered by the Rev. D. W. Morgan BA. Evertoa Brow, Liverpool, and the success of the first speaks for the anticipation of the next one. Mr. D. J. Price will occupy the chair. Warrington Ex-Registrar's Death.

We regret to announce the dents which took place veeterday morning at Bath, of Major Edward Joseph ..:icholson, who was at one time a pmmiment solicitor in the town and also registrar at the Warrington County Court. The deceased gentleman was only years of age and had just returned from a Voyage in the East, Japan and India being the places he visited. While in Warrington he was a member of the firm of Messrs. Nicholson, White and Nicholson, and was recognised as a very successful County Court advocate and sound lawyer. About thirty years ago be succeeded his father as Registrar at the County Curt until his retirement some few years ago, when Mr. T. J. Ridgway was appointed He was also clerk to the Warrington Water Works Company and was largely instrumental in bringing about its municipalisation. He held a commission in the Warrington Militia until a few years back and leaves a widow a son and a daughter to mourn his loss. Children's Home Concert.

Academy-street Adult School. On Sunday morning last Mr. W. Webster gave a most descriptive and all-absorbing leourette on " Ants." He tactfully made use of expressions that were readily understOod and yet at the same time moet interesting and educative. An especially striking contrast was made in the ingenuity of the ant as c..mpared with man. Even the erection of the Egyptian Pyramids we., insignificant in comparison to the. work accomplished by these insects. Mr. Webster's personal resea,ch and his assiduous oath- Nineos and calculations at Frodsham and the Solidi of England, created astonishment and no entertaining was the lecturette that at the conclusion of the meet- ing it was decided to arrange an expedition into the country for both the Adult schools, Mr. Webster volunteering to provide elementary instruction in botany and entomology.

Fire In Bewsej-street. The residence of Dr. Adams in Bewsey-rnsd was on Monday evening the scene of an outbreak of lire which fortunately was subdued before it obtained serious dimenaiona. It appears that about seven ' o'clock a light was accidently dropped in an upper I room, setting the clothing on fire. An alarm won at once rained, and Dr. and Mrs. Adams and several members of the household endeavoured to subdue the flames. The smoke, however, drove them for • time out of the room, and the Dr. at once telephoned for the Fire Brigade. In the meantime Mr.. Adams and the maid pluckily continued their efforts, and when the brigade, under the direction of Superintendent Turner, arrived on the scene the flame+ were .10011 maatered with a few buckets of water, the fire oppli- , ances not being used. Only alight damage was done to the fixtures and clothing.

Warrington Photographic Society. On Tuesday Mr. H. Mills, of Bolton, gave a most instructive lecture on "Artistic mounting of photo- graphic prints." The find section of hie sulrject on the needful trimming might be sumnaed up in the lecturer's aphorism " Spare the knife, and spoil the picture." He gave an actual instance of a half-plate negative cut down to half-inch by three-eighths. Then a piee was found worth enlarging. end a good result was obtained. A minor detail, and yet of importance in some subjects, such as snow pictures and flat' lighted portraits, is the colour of the glans used in framing—ordinary picture glass giring such a green tint as to spoil these delicate print.. Again, no picture ought to be centrally mounted. It gives an appearance of want of stability, a kind of "slipped- I down " effect, only to be corrected by raising the ' print above the middle of the mount. The narrowest margin must be at the top; a wide. space at the sides, and still wider at the bottom. The lecturer classes piebtres mounted in corners, is 'slip-in" mounta,and I such other atrocities, in one group— the fire-lighters. A very pleasant evening concluded with a hearty vote of thanks. Mr. A. Davis was in the chair. Ccrtear, Jofteph Rodeo's k Sons' celebrated knives and forks, etc.—Agent, J. S. GARLAND, Ironmonger, Bridge at., Warrington.—Advt. Coming of Age Celebrations. LASS,

1901-1910

cannot pretend to discuss the merits of regenerative gas furnaces against direct coal firing, or to make comparison of the speed at which German machines can melt the tope of wine glass against those used in our own country. But experts tell us that while, for some classes of glass, Germany has ready access to better raw material than we have, the main advantage is in the methods of working and the science and skill which has been directed to the industry. But they tell us further that those engaged in the glass industry in this country are rapidly regaining their supremacy in the different classes of glass manufacture, this with the possible exception of glass for scientific appliances in which the Germans have established a pre-eminence which is almost unchallenged. Our diagram reveals a healthy trade gain over the whole trade of £1,119,000 which is a striking contrast to the figures of the last decade which revealed an increase of imports ot £94.0,t44 and of exports only £20,1/0i showing a trade less of £920,159.

BEWSEY SHOOTING FATALITY.

Death of Well-knoAn Liverpool Gentleman.

We regret to announce the (lean, of Mr. E. Cleaver, of Devonshire House, Sefton Park, Liverpool, which occurred at the Warrington Infirmary on Wednesday rooming, consequent upon injuries he sustained in as shooting accident on Lord Lilford's Bewsey estate, on January 30Th. It will be remembered that on January 306. )Jr. Cleaver was out shooting on the Bewsey estate when he W. accidentally shot in the face. He was conveyed to the Warrington Infirmary where at first he seemed to he recovering, hut, relapse followed and he succumbed. Mr. Cleaver, who was years of age, was the youngest son of the late Mr. ft itliam Cleaver, and he was a familiar figure in Liverpool, where he was greatly esteemed. Be was a well-known sportsman, and had spent 29 years of his life in the East where he had figured as a hunter and shooter of big game. Be was for a long time in the service of the Bombay Trading Corporation and had been for some years superintendent at Rangoon. Ile had also been in charge of the Corporation teak forests in Burma's. He retired some little time ago and went to reside with his brother in Devonshire-road, Liverpool.

A STEADY TRADE GAIN.

[By C. E. PARKER. No. 5.1

The Glass trade affords one of the few instances where the imports of manufac- tured articles are greater than the exports. The difference, however, has been getting steadily less during the last ten years, and adding the decrease of imports to the increase of exports we have a total trade gain of £1,119,000. In the previous decade the trade balance was the other way, the increase of imports being greater than the increase of exports by £920,159. This afforded a con- venient argument for Tariff Reformers which has been worked for all it is worth in Warrington. In the glass trade as in others the effect of time is crumbling every illustra- tion which formed a foundation for this Tariff Reform.

It is somewhat risky for one not an expert to offer any opinion on the reasons why the users of glass find it to their interest and advantage to buy foreign glass goods. We

A MAIDEN SPEECH.

Mr. Smith, MP., apologises for the Corporation Bill. YORKSHIRE MEMBERS CRITICISMS.

Li h ilouna of Commons on Thursday, Mr. Harold Smith, the Member for Warrington, mails his maiden speech. , On the motion for the third reading of the War- rington Corporation Bill, suspended from last session Mr. Booth (Pontelrsct) cpposed the bill on the ground that it coetaineil two rlauees which, in hiat view, seriously interfered with the liberty of the subject. The first of these, clause 22, required a gas , consumer in the borough to give twenty-four hours' notice in writing of his intention to terminate his tenancy. It .R 8 preposterons that poor people should be compelled to write a letter if they wished to cod their contract for the supply of gas. when one remembered that in Lancashire almost all the important contracts, particularly in the cotton trade, were made by word of mouth. lathe case of a private firm, a condition if the kind embodied in this clause might be waived ; but such was the

lEfIV 1-1 It lON V Su3ll, ,0111. CR VrIoN I Irril 141, there would be no chance of soy such c flsideration being shown by the corporation. The second clause to which he objected provided that ice cream should not be manufactured, sold, or stored in any sleeping room or in any room which was in a condition likely to render the community injurious to health. Solytdy I wanted ice cream to be made under such conditions ; but if these conditions prevailed they were

to the Corporation of Warrington, which had sufficient powers under the etisting law to deal with them. There was much insanitary property in Warrington, and it would not meet the caw/ to prosscuie some ' ignorant Italian for selling the icecream it the build- ing in which it was made was allowed to remain. Way should little children be brought up in such rooms ? Why should hoot be cooked in them ? In too many cases it was because the men who drew the rents sat on the heal authorities. He could not imagine that the new member for Warrington wnold like to introduce himself to public life by defending such clauses as these. MR. SMITH'S APPEAL.

Mr. Harold Smith, addressing the house for the first time, asked leave as the representative of the borough which bad spent much time and money in promoting the bill, to reply to the hon. member. He did not quarrel with the criticism that had been made, because he knew the hon. member was eminently sincere, and because he had had no previous °ppm- tnnitv of raising questions on which lie felt keenly. But be oold liKe to recall the history of the bill. since December. Itsrd, it had been slowly wending its way to the Statute Bo d: It passed through all its stages save one I,efore the disiolution, and it was 1.041P1V hat ofa formality that it should have again to come before the licrise, lii thew circumstances. he

before objecting at its final stage. On the merito of the ease made Itj• the hon member he sympathised with him. and if he were considering the details of ouch a bill and these points mere raised, his poor services would I, entirely at the hon.mernber's disposal. Be entirely agreed that it wav oppressive on the part of a corpor tion to insert that tenants of gis-tneters should gave notice in writing. and it might work injustice in the case of poor, illiterate people. DA THE CLERGY OF ST. ANN'S curr,cii.

St. Ann's Church and its numerous organization. are doing a much-appreciated work on Evangelical • lines, to one of the poorest .bstriets in Warrington. The Vicar (Rev. James Davies) does not confine his energ;es to parochial effsiirs but is conspicuously associated with the Church and educational life of the town generally. He taken a particularly keen interest in the maintenance of the Church day schools and in addilion to holding tie secretaryship of the Church of ' England Schools Foundation Managers' Association, he is a co-opted member of the Warrington Education Committee. Mr. Davies is a native of Cardigan and received his early education in Cardiff and Carmarthen. Ile gained his fret experi- ence of parochial activities with the Rev. David Dl)., subsequently Dean of St. David's. Alter .ome years spent in edneational work, he entered the London College of Divinity, Flighbury, London, under the Principal, Rev. Dr. Waller, for ispecial theological instruction and training for the stry of the Church of England. He gained the Bible prize in the last year of his residence, and passed the Universities' Pieliminary examination /or Holy Orders. Mr. Davies was ordained deacon in 1857 and priest in 18ss, by the late Right Rev. J. C. Ryle, Dl)., Lord Bishop of Liverpool. For six years he held a curacy at Emmanuel, Everton. Liveri.ol, :Ind gained the cordial good-will of the parishioners. He came to St. Ann's, War- rington, in September, 1893, to take charge of the parish for the Lord Bishop of Liverpool, on the Vicar being granted a licence of non-residence. On leaving Liverpool he was the recipient of a valuable cheque and silver picket Communion service from the people amongst whom he hail lablurel no successfullyin May, 1590, the living of St. Anii'm became vacant through the death of the Vicar. and Mr. Davies was appointed to succeed hint by the patrons (the Simeon Trusteev), on the nomination of the Bishop. Under Mr. Davies's direction, many notable improvements have been effected in the parish. The church hun been renovated

and tastefully decorated, ?de day .ehool buildings and yards thoroughly overhauled, an.l finally, as a memorial to the late Queen Victoria, a new vicarage wan erected in Orford Avenue ut a cost of .V 2.600. There are many useful and healthy organisations, including Home and Foreign Mission- ary Association P.A.S. anal C.M.S 1, Scripture Union, Adult and Junior Temperance Societies. District Visitors and Magazine Distributors. Mothers' Meeting, Sowers' and Reapers' Band and Communi- cants L. nit,. Mr. Davies leas one of the promoters of the Junior Clerical Bible and Prayer Union in connection with the Church Pastoral Aid Society in Liverpool, and was, while in Liverpool, on the Execurive Committee, lie was also a promoter of the C.M.S. Junior Clergy Union for the Diocese of Liverpool, and was on the Executive Committee for many years, in 1898 being appointeri representative to the CM S. Junior Clergy Union Conference hell at Derby. He assisted in the formation of the Warring- ton and District Band of Hope Union, atel was at one time chairman of the Executive Committee. Fo,. many years he has been hon. secretary for the Winwick Deanery for the Church Pastoral Aid Society. The Rev. F. C. Shirtcliff, who entered upon his emery at St. Ann's in 1910, was educated at St. Catherine's College. Cambridge. He 11,9 ordained deacon in 1888 and priest in 1559 by the Bishop of Liverpool; his first curacy being at the Church of St. Mary Nlagdalene, Liverpool. Since then he has worked in the following parishes —Pemberton, 1888- ill; St. Peter's, Warrington, 189'2 ; St. Polyearp's, Evertor., 1843 Si. C.snon W Mi.) made some remarks regarding drunkenness in Wsrrington, when preaching et the Parish Church on Sunday even- ing. lie had seen some extracts from the Chief Oinstablehi report, he said, and it appeared to hire Canon Willi. the, IVnrrinaton held an unenviable notoriety as regarded dmnkeoness. A route march took plane on Saturday, February I I th in the Appleton District, ma Lower Walton, by th members•of the above Company, under the command of Major J. Brookes, and Captain and Adjutant I. Seeker. After haversack rations were issued and inspected, the Company left headquerters at 2.30 p.m , 28 in number, end proceeded in toe direction of Appleton Church, when on arrival et the Dingle the company were .opposed to be in hostile country, nod were divided up ea follows r—Advance Guard and Main Body. Tte whole Advance Guard was under the command of Staff Sergeant Essentrigh, and the Main Body under Sergetort 11 Leach. Sergeant Rates was in chstge of the Van Guard and Staff Sergeant Essenhigh in °tattle of the Main Guard.Pointets end contenting flies were also used and the sigaallera under Staff Sergeant Teat were put out SS liana., The whole arrangements were enter the charge of Sergeant instructor Bottotnley. the cyclists were used to carry dispatches and were told to prom .d cautiously to a farm ty Appleton Church to get water for the purpose of making tea. CHRISTENING OF EARL FITZWILLIANS SON.

Earl Filzwilliam's baby son, the first direct heir born to the head of the House of Wentworth for seventy-one years, was christened cn Saturday amid scenes of great jubilation. The rejoicings were on an elaborate scale, over 7,000 guests being invited. Earl Fitzwilliani is enormously rich, and his country seat, Wentworth Woodhouse, near Sheffield, is the largest private mansion in the countly. The family tide dales back So the Conquest. when, after the battle of Hastings, William the Conqueror presented the first Earl with a scarf from his own neck, and this scarf was worn by the baby at the cbristen'ng. Our photograph shows the Earl and Countess and their four daughters, the Ladies Omaha, Ellrieda, Joan and Helena FitzwilliAn.

Examiner, Saturday February 18 1911.

THE NATURAI. LINE OP OUR INDUSTRIAL DEVELOPMENT

citizens awoke in time to its immense import- ance in the life of the old town, and kept the vandal horde at bay; but the plans for widening our main streets might each have been expressly fixed in order to remove as many ancient monuments as possible.

WHAT MADMAN FIRST SUGGESTED that the only way to get from Buttermarket to Mersey-street was through the heart of the second Academy? The Old Fox is ' doomed to go—we would that it might be re-erected—but places like the Barley Mow, the quaint old inns and cottages in Church- street, the Plague House, Latchford, the Black Horse and the Clapgates Farm ; should all be spared, and we are glad indeed to find, in the Town Planning regulations, that special attention is called to the import- ance of preserving places of historic interest or natural beauty. . . . .

The phrase includes, of course,our gardens, parks, and open spaces. Bank Park, Victoria Park, Queen's Gardens, and the front part of St. Elphin's Park have fortunately all been saved from the hands of the spoiler. Orford Hall should be treated with not less respect. Its history and its inherent picturesqueness each entitle it to perpetuity. . _ .

The preservation (Cr Bevisey, the quaint old manor house whose half-filled moat and mouldering walls enshrine the town's most moving and romantic memories, should certainly be marked upon the plan as sacro- sanct. Bruche, Appleton and Sankey Halls, the Roman Camp, the Ackers Pits, the Dingle, the Hill Warren and a dozen other places come within the same category, The true Town Planners all agree that suitable localitiet should be reserved for industrial purposes, preferable in a. place where they are least conspicuous, and where the prevailing winds will bear the smoke (there should not really be any) away from instead of right amidst the residential popu- lation. Dila is obviously the eastern portion of the borough ; but

appears to be along the river and the Ship Canal. The Docks which we still some day hope to see,locate themselves most naturally by the old river bend at Wilderspool, with possible developments between Howley and the Latchford Lock. In the meantime, the river bend would make a charming place for boating. The railways are immediately contiguous to this locality; and it is safe to, say that, on the whole, the big new works which will come to Warrington will naturally be attracted to an area so conveniently approached by rail, by road, and by water. And these new industries should all be care- fully regulated. Noxious trades should be tabooed ; and no works whatever should be permitted to offend too glaringly against the elementary canons of good taste: . . .

With these main feaures well in mind, we can easily conceive

A SKELETON PLAN OF THE NEW WARRINGTON Broad main roads, radiating out in all directions from the town, and connected, at reasonable intervals, by concentric roads of reasonable width, diversified by trees and strips of grass, with separate sections for the various kinds of traffic ; the location of the

industrial quarter in the best and most con venient localities; the reservation of resi- dential areas suitable for all classes of the population; the preservation of ancient land- marks and all existing parks and open spaces; the grouping of the principal muni- ,cip9l buildings in a monumental central area; the monumental treatment of important centres—these strike us as the fundamental features in the plan of the New Warrington. MANY MINOR DETAILS.

will at once occur to all our readers. The desirability of so arranging our new streets as to make them end, wherever possible, in a pleasant vista—the Church spire, the To es n Hall, some stately tower or handsome build- ing ; better still, a distant glimpse of hills and woods and fields—will probably com- mend itself, at once, to everybody who has noticed the effect of this device in continental cities. The provision of such lakes as that at Stockton Heath, for swimming, boating, fishing, or merely for the pleasure of feasting one's eyes upon a calm expanse of water, is another great desideratum, and many suit- able sites at once suggest themselves. We have mentioned one already. The best and most convenient sites should be selected

for our elementary schools, and special sites should be reserved for a new Grammar School and a High School for Girls. More congruous architecture, relevant to local history and conditions; the limita- tion of the caprices of the individual builder; harmony of general treatment with a reason- able diversity of detail; more elasticity in laying out the meaner streets—these and a . . . .

hundred other details will no doubt at once occur to all our readers. The future of the town—its health, its comfort, its convenience, its attractiveness—affects them all, and all of them should lend a hand in shaping it. We cordially invite them to co-operate with the responsible Committee in the splendid and inspiring task which lies before them.

We want to pool ideas on a matter of such immense importance. W. shall welcome any suggestions that may be sent to us and see that they are duly communicated to the proper quarter. Already we have made tremendous strides towards the new, the better, and the brighter Warrington which we set out to build. But much remains to be done, and we ask our friends to assist us in creating that enlightened public opinion without which the best Committees in the world are powerless, but fortified by which the town we love may rise to all the height of its great opportunity and realize the dream which has inspired us in our daily toil for over twenty years. WA regret that our contributor A.M. has een obliged to undergo a slight operation and has not recovered sufficient strength for the preparation of his usual article. A continuation of "The Battle of Land and Sea" may be looked for in our next issue.

ED. " Ex."

FIVAIIRIP-4VTON CHAffERS. A NEW WARRINGTON.

WHAT MIGHT BE DONE BY TOWN PLANNING.

"The Sunrise Annual "—a journal which is continuing the good work done by " Sunrise" and "The Dawn"—contains a! suggestive article on the possibilities of creating a now Warrington under the powers given by the Housing and Town Planning Act—" the best and greatest achievement of the late Government," to quote the words of the writer of the article, whom one has no difficulty in identifying as Alderman Bennett.

"The task of launching a Town Planning Scheme for Warrington rests primarily upon the shoulders of the Street Improvement and Lighting Committee, a name which hardly seems to fit the Committee's present duties; but their work is so responsible and far- reaching that, if they are wise, they ought to gladly welcome help from any quarter; and they might do well to invite the assistance of the local architects, the Warrington and Beautiful Warrington Societies, and any other individuals or institutions whose knowledge or experience might be useful in the task they have before them. This seems to us essentially a case in which co-option might be tried with great advantage. A thoroughly expert and capable official should be put in charge of the depart- ment; and whilst we are profoundly anxious that the New Warrington should, as far as possible, be the work of Warring- ton brains and hands, it may be necessary, having regard to the complexities of the itiestion and our comparative inexperience in dealing with them to obtain, from time to time, that expert assistance from outside which is now readily available. The Com- mittee have, with admirable wisdom, done their best to qualify themselves for the great work they have in hand, and several of the leading members and officials have had the privilege of attending various important Conferences upon the subject, and visiting the principal Garden Cities and Suburbs in pursuit of information; whilst the chairman, Alderman Bennett, went, on their behalf, in the Autumn of last year, to several leading towns in France, Germany, and Austria, as a member of one of the Town Planning parties organized with so much spirit and success by the National Housing and Town Planning Council, The Committee have, in fact, been quietly preparing for their new duties for some months past, and we believe that they are now engaged in definitely considering the preliminaries of a comprehensive scheme.

===== 0002601_19110218_art0083.txt | included=true | included =====
WHAT ARE THE FUNDAMENTALS

they should keep in mind? At least as long ago as 1495, as an old plan of Warrington attests, the general scheme consisted of four main streets crossing each other in the centre of the town almost at right angles, and running, roughly speaking, to the four points of the compass. The plan persisted, as an illustra- tion shows, right through the stormy era of the Civil War; and the four quaint, narrow thoroughfares which met at Market Gate came down, almost unchanged, to many of us in our childhood, with all their pleasant memories of by-gone times. These streets and all the main approaches to the town are being gradually widened—in our opinion, even yet, they are not wide enough—but the essential features of the town's arrangement have not changed. Before the Act was passed, at our suggestion, thirteen years ago, the city fathers had the wit to fix upon a Circus as

===== 0002601_19110218_art0084.txt | included=true | included =====
THE CENTRAL FEATURE

in the plan of the New Warrington, which, even then, began to slowly shape itself in a few progressive minds. They made it toler- ably spacious, and, more recently, they had the wisdom to insist upon a uniform design. Unluckily there have been several deviations from the original plan, which, we are prom.' to think, was the work of a Warrington firm of architects (Messrs. J. E. Wright, Garnett and Wright) ; but, although its grace and relevance are thereby marred, the earliest segment of the Circus, which, as we write, is rapidly approaching com- pletion, gives promise of a very dignified and notable result. With Market Gate as the focus of the town, and the four streets radiating from it, north and south and east and west, the general outlines of a comprehensive plan instinctively suggest themselves, and might be best defined, perhaps, by the illustration of a spider's web. The problem is, of course, considerably complicated by the Ship Canal, the river, and the intersecting railway ; but, subject to the necessary qualifications which these obstacles impose, the aim should be to make new radial roads wherever needed,with some definite and direct objective—say Leigh and St. Helens, for example—and to link up these, as well as the existing thoroughfares, by suitable concentric roads, of ample width and dignified arrangement. There ought,for instance, to be roads from Padgate, and from Fearnhead Cross to Orford, and thence through Longford and through Buttonwood to Sankey. These might easily be made to connect with Manchester-road, and should be ultimately linked with the attrac- tive suburbs of the town which are

developing so rapidly beyond the Ship Canal. Al present, there are not any roads from Lancashire into Cheshire between the great Swing Bridge at Latchford and the lofty cantilever Bridge at Warburton, or between

' the Bridge upon the Cheater-road and the Transporter Bridge at Runcorn. Warring- ton is sadly short of bridges. Of course there . . . . .

ought to be a bridge not less than sixty feet in width from Howley to Victoria Park connecting with a new through road from Latchford to Wilders- pool ; a great Embankment running from Bridge Foot to the Weir ; and a great new avenue running right from the heart of Cheshire to the front of the Town Hall. The men who planned Winmarleigh-street, so narrow and so badly-centred, and who allowed the London and North Western Railway Company to build the ugly and lop- sided Bridge right at the bottom end of it which is not in tho middle and effectually shuts out the once refreshing view of Walton and Hill Cliff, deserved to be " hanged, drawn and quartered," and to go down to posterity side by side with the architects of Scotland-road ALL PICTURESQUE 4)R ANCIENT BUILDINGS should be carefully 'marked out upon the new Town Plan, and should, wherever possible, be reverently preserved. In many of our late improvements this consideration has utterly been lost sight of, and, in a town which absolutely teemed with ancient, pictureque, and often most historic houses, only a dwindling handful now remain. The Old Academy escaped by accident, because it happened to stand a little way back from Bridge-street, and because a few enlightened

===== 0002601_19110218_art0086.txt | included=true | included =====
FROM LABOUR'S STANDPOINT.

BI Philip Snowden, I.P.

WHAT SHOULD M.P.'S BE PAID!

The Labour Party's bad luck in the ballot for private Bills has taken away all hope that their Bill for the reversal of the Osborne decision will get a second reading debate. There still remain two possibilities. The Government may introduce a Bill of their own or they may do as they did last session with the Suffrage Bill—give special facilities for the consideration of the Labour Party's Bill. At the time I write there is some faint hope that the Government may bring in their own Bill on this subject,but it is only a faint hope, because the Government Whips I have got the idea that the Veto Bill, Supply, the Budget, the Bills mentioned in the King's Speech, will occupy all theketime of the session. The Coronation, it is understood, will make a break of some weeks in the Parliamentary work of the year.

===== 0002601_19110218_art0087.txt | included=true | included =====
UNEASY LIBERAL MEMBERS.

The Bill for the reversal of the law in regard to trade unions and politics, whether it be a Government or a private member's Bill, need not occupy very much time. It is true that the question is a highly contro- versial one,and that there will be very strong otiposition to the Labour Party's Bill from both Liberal and Unionist members; but the point of the controversy is a very narrow one, and when once the principle is settled, the details need not occupy much time. The Government, if they still hold to the state- ment on the subject made by Mr. Asquith last November, cannot bring forward a Bill which will be satisfac- tory to the Labour Party. If they do introduce a measure on the lines indicated by the Prime Minister, then there will be a good deal of uneasiness among the Liberal members. I knew that this Osborne ques tion played a prominent part in the recent elections in the industrial centres, but I am surprised to learn that so many M.P.'s have given written pledges to support the Labour Party's demand, even if it involves voting against the Government. I do not think, however, that the number of supporters pledged to go with the Labour Party on this question is large enough to turn the scale against the Government, as the Unionist party would vote almost solidly against 'the Labour Party's demand for the restitution of the old position. THE BEST PLAN.

I still think it would be the best plan for the Government to leave this an open ques- tion, and let the House of Commons decide what sort of a Bill it would prefer to pass. I cannot see how the Government can refuse to deal with the question this session, or refuse to give time for others to do so. The Prime Minister had admitted the urgency of the question. Things cannot be allowed to remain as they are at present. Trade Unions are openly doing what the law has declared to be illegal. The Labour Party is an illegal organisation. This is an absurd situation, and Parliament for the credit of the law must interfere at once. The Prime Minister told the Trades Union deputation the other day that he intended to deal with the payment of members this session. He knows full well that this reform will not meet the trade union difficulty. The trade unions are demanding that political action shall be made one of the lawful activities of the Unions, and this is the concession which must be made during the present session of Parliament. It rests with the Government to devise the way and the means. THE PREMIER AND THE SUFFRAGE.

===== 0002601_19110218_art0088.txt | included=true | included =====
The Trades Union deputation, to which

dang,

have referred, which nth the Prime Minister last week to submit to him the resolutions of the Trades Union Congress on electoral reform did not get very much satisfaction. The Premier, however, did make one very interesting confession which the women suffragists will doubtless duly note. Mr. Asquith declared himself in favour of man- hood suffrage, with a short and transferrable residential qualification. He is well known to be a strong opponent of women's suffrage, although he does not disdain to use the work of women in his own constituency to help to secure his return. About three years ago he promised a deputation of Liberals that he would bring forward a Reform Bill before the Parliament expired. Two of his Parliaments have expired since then, but no Reform Bill has appeared. It would now appear that Mr. Asquith has in his mind the proposal to adopt Manhood Suffrage. The Government, lie said, three years ago, would not oppose an amendment to extend the franchise to women; but Mr. Asquith knows full well that there is not a majority in the House of Commons in favour of adult suffrage, although he•could com- mand a majority in support of Manhood Suffrage. Mr. Asquith evidently hopes to "dish"the women suffragists by this proposal for Manhood Suffrage. The same thing has I happen(' before, both in this and other countries. On the continent, the women have been elbowed out when there was a chance of getting more votes for men. It was so in this country in 1832, in 1867, and in 1884. All this makes it of vital importance that the women should get inside the fran- chise, however few in numbers, before the next general Reform Bill is brought forward. Then it will be impossible to ignore them, They would have to be given the same terms as men in any scheme of citizen suffrage. PAYMENT OF MEMBERS.

The Trades Union deputation called the atk.ntion of Mr. Asquith to the question of (payment of Members. I doubt if it is wise for individual members of the Labour Party ( commit themselves at this stage to precise us in regard to this matter. It is a case ( re the trade unions and the Labour Party t well content themselves just now by ng the principle upon the Covernment leaving to them the task of putting for- a definite scheme. The precise amount payment is a matter on which y be wide difference of opinion. ns are suggesting £l5O a year. favour sums ranging up to £5OO a Chairman of the Labour Confer- as his opinion that £3OO a year y pass would be sufficient. The is such a diversity of opinion e payment should be shows that Me to wait and see, aul then atter in the light of full knoi- are two aspects of tffis yment of Members. It may as a proposal to compen- r the out-of-pocket expenses , or it may be considered for their time and services. union at present seems to be nt fixed should be only stiffi- the expenses of being in Parlia- the member to live upon his leans, or to work for a living in ion or occupation. There is a be made out for this view, but deal to be said in favour of a

payment which would give the country

claim upon the full time-of the Member dur- ing the sittings of Parliament. I have an open mind on the subject, but I am sure that .if Parliament should decide to pay members, in addition to compensating them, that the dangers which some people seem to fear from payment of members would not result. I am inclined to think that there would be a better chance of getting the best men as candidates if members were paid for their services. The fear of the political adventurer is groundless. If members were paid, there would be a far wider field of selection open to Constituencies. At present, the ..ian who can find the most money is preferred to the poor man of ability. Even in the Labour Party the same thing applies to a great extent. The candi- date who can tell a Selection Committee that his trade union is prepared to find the money for his election expenses and for his main- tenance in Parliament stands a far better chance of being adopted than another who has little financial backing, and whose candidature would involve the local move- ment in the responsibility of finding a good part of the expenses of the contest; although the latter individual might be infinitely the better man from every point of view.

===== 0002601_19110218_art0089.txt | included=true | included =====
THE MAN ON THE MAKE.

Payment of Meinbers would not help the political 'man on the make" to get into Parliament more easily than he can to-day. A man is not returned to Parliament simply because he asks to go there. He requires a constituency to elect him. With the wider range of selection which Payment of Members would give, the political parties would be more likely to select men of proved worth and ability. If a constituency did elect a " bounder," then it is the fault of the electors. They ought to know a man before

placing him in the responsible position of a Member of Parliament. There are dangers in adopting Payment of Members, but the fear that the quality and character of candi- dates would deteriorate is not One Or the dangers.
