A MYSTERY OF MAYFAIR,

JOIN STRANGE WINTER.

CIIAPTER I. TIM BOURN IN MIVIIN

tt man a dark and dreary night, the driasle Ind slush inorca6ing the misery of the poverty - atriken street, down which trudged a nian in a well-worn and moth-eaten over- coat, which clung to his ill-fed body, show- ing every line of his slim figure. Ilis boots were nearly soleless, and he slid rather than walked the muddy pavement, pat rows of evil-looking dwellings such as gave him the heart-ache. The' Seven Dials is not a neigh- bourhood any man would select for his abode unless driven by atress of circumstances to seek some harbour of refuge, no matter how uninviting. Turning up the frayed velvet collar of his coat, and Jamming hie hat over his eyes, he showed by thin simple action how weak and depressed he felt. So great was his fatigue that he could barely drag one leg after the other, yet he persevered, for he must rest his heed itionewhere. With the back of his hand—slender, white, ant deli- cately fortned—be wiped away the rain drops from his eyelashes, the better to distinguish the numbers chalked on the sun blistered and paintlese door.. 'the wind-blown flame flickering inside the blurred glass aided him somewhat. 58, at last. I shall never know the in- fernal house, they are all so mach alike," maid he, eyeing the bricks and mortar from top to bottom. "Not a light burning in any of the rooms. My delightful fellow lodgers are on their usuaL nightly prowl, picking up any trifle that happens to come within their reach. DEATH or Mn. C. W. PutuTes.—We regret to announce the death, at his residence, the Ring-W-Belle, Daresbury, of Mr. Charles William Phillips, who was licencee of the above hotel for over 27 }rare. The deceased gentleman had been ill for about eight weeks tottering front inflammation of the brain, bet hi, death came sea greet shock to his many friends. Mr. Phillip. who was burn at Godstone (Surrey) was formerly in the employ of Sinners. Penmen and Knowles, Cu, Ltd., and on retirement from that Hem took charge of the Ring-o,Bells hotel. In politica he was an ardent Conservative, and wee Secretary to the Daresbory Habitation of the Primrose League. Ile was corres- pondent of the Daresbury County School, and had also been for many years a Freemason. As a church- man he held various office', at Daresbury Church. The funeral took place at Daresbury Church on Tuesday afternoon and, despite the heavy rain, many mourners assembled to pay their last respects to Mr. Phillip.. DIVISION

An entbuisatic gathering of the supporters of Mr. J. A. Seddon, en-Ill'. for the Newton Division, met at Oolborne on Saturday night, when there won an interesting series of presentations. At a gathering in the Congregational schools Miss Marie Lowe, on behalf of the young people of Golhorne, presented to Mr. and Mra. HO remarked that Mr. Seddon had rendered invaluable service both to the Nesseon Division and to the country in general. (Cheers.) Mr. Seddon, in returning thanks for the presentations, said they were not celebrating a victory. list defeat bad always been the testing time, both for men and natio. and alter the great surprise of the 7th of December last, when their opponents were almost as surprised as themselves, he was more than touched by the grim determination made on the spot by his loyal supporters that what they had done in the past would pale into insignificance as compared with the heroic efforts they would make to wipe out the defeat of that night. The other people had bad celehrat ions— celebrated their final (cheers and laughter.. The Town Clerk's Salary.

Sir—l see our wise Town Council has again been making bistory and showing its ability to be generous at the public expense. It appears that our Town Clerk has completed his majority as clerk of War- rington and it is the wish of three-fourths of the Town Council that he have a testimonial for his services, add they have therefore voted him an increase of salary of £2lOlB year Gilt of the borough funds. This is magnificently liberal, tut it would have been far mole creditable on their Curt if thy bad subscribed something out i I their ow n pocketa.than to dip their bands into that of the already over- burdened ratepayer. The opinion was expressed at the Council meeting by his admirer, that the Town Clerk ought to have a ealary of ‘,.:1.0011 a year. One is struck by the anxiety of the majority of one Aldermen awl Councillore to pat upon the back the higher officials and to raise their salaries without a murmur, but when it comes to the lower ranks of employees they seem to be struck with pangs of remorse and -alk about the rise of rates and exceeding the standard rate of wages. As was memioned in the recent deirate, and as most people will recollect, what a cry of pious horror was raised when sonic months ago the scavengers had the temerity to ask Ur a rise of a shrilling a week. Surely these wi,e Aldermen and Councillors can strain at a gnat and swallow anniel. The Council dces not even honour its own signature. for is there not a Standing Order to the effect that no employee of the Corporation over 21 years of age shall receive less than 211. per week, yet I am informed that there are several (aid below this scale. All who have had occasion to walk about our streets during the recent dry weather will have noticed the insufficient scavenging that has been done, yet I am told that a certain number of men employed is the department are only allowed to work alternate weeks. Where were the boasted workingmen Conn- cillore in the recent debate ? Will they now demand that the lower ranks shall receive equal treatment with the higher ? Many well-wishers to the good old town will regret ilie absence of Mr. Norbury Williams and the late Mr. Jame. Doe:de—Yours A-c..

Lori" Roberts and the Territorials. Sir;—Will you kiodly allow one to reply to your able leader of last Saturday's issue? In this leader you state that the Army most he managed not from the public platform or by the National Service League, Nast by those who are responsible. Let me point out rthat it Is not the management of the Army which Lord Roberts and the National Service League are criticising. We have always maintained that Mr. Haldane has done marvels with the voluntary prin- ciple. What we are asserting in season and out of season,is that just as civil education tents never Ratio- factorywntil it we. made compuleory, no Our military education will never he satisfactory lipid made compulsory. For the simple reason that until this is done neither enough time nor enough earnestness can be put in the business. In order to get the nation to realize this feet it is ab,olutely necessary, however distasteful, to criticise, not the nianagement, but the results of our present military institution. Hence what you designate as the crabbing of the ten itorials by Lord Roberta. Hue gallant t ield Marshal is in fact, attacked just as those other great men have been, who in former years, strove to arouse their countet men out of their apathy and ignor- ance on matte's of National Defence. Baron Stoffell, the Freneli attaclu, at Berlin. who warned his Government from Itit;:i to le7o of the inefficiency of the French army as compared with the tierman, lint all in vain, was virtually kicked out of Ids Club when he came back to Paris. This simply for telling the truth. In our own time the most cruel idiellbe was heaped on I ieneral Butler for telling us the facts of the case about the Boer War. At a time when in defensive matters certainly " the prophets prophesy falsely and the people love to have it no" that man is indeed precious who discerns the truth and is not afraid to led it to the I eople. Lord Robert., Lord Wolseley. Sir Evelyn Word, Sir George White, Admiral Sir Gerard Noel and ben- deeds of other naval and military men, who have left the services and ate free to week openly. are per:claiming that National Service is necessary for the safety and welfare of our country, and they are proving their case not by mere ipse dixits hut by hard facts and clear logic. Their argument ins nutshell is this: that it is better and safer to have two lines of defence instead n f only one. The old rnotto of not putting all your eggs in one basket applies as much to-day trait uid Dal years ego. They coint out that given the trained manhood of the youth of the nation we shall not become suddenly panic stricken and demaud Dreadnoughts by the dozen and loans by the millions; we shall not lower nor imperial prestige by having to concentrate all oar ships an the North Sea ; and we shall strengthen and fortify the moral and physical strength of the people upou which in the ultimate analysis the serviyal of nations depends. You make a great point of nor existing second line of defence. You refer to the magnitude of tV com- bined forces of the repulses and the territorials. In military matters. however. it is never wise in take the most favourable eircunistann e. The time when we are likely to be its danger is not when the whole of our regular army in at home, but when it is suddenly called away. In that case we ehould be left with 250.0, I territorials inadequately traitted and inadequately officered. This forte trill have to pro- vide garrisons for Ireland, for our seaports and large lowan. protection for cur railways and the field army which w ill have to meet the 71010., which those in authority now admit is the possible number that might slip through. And those 711,000 will be thoroughly trained and thoroughly officered. Ask any soldier who is fiee to give yeu an impartial ,pinion as to whether there :..0.11(S) would suffice to perform the above duties and he will laugh at you for asking such an absurd question.—Yours GEORGE CROSFIELD.

Mr. G. K. Chesterton and the "Coming Slavery."

Sir,—Mcst of those who listened to Mr. Chesterton in the Co-operative Hall would probably admit to being amused rather than it.structed. An undercurrent , of sly humour characterised hi,. address, and suggested a suspicion that he was poking fun at his audience. Taking him, however, mote seriously than perhaps he intended to be taken, I would like to criticise two p iota which lie made. In auggestmg he hardly did more than suggest—that Socialism would mean slaveiy. I think he made the mistake of ...lining that the present State—which might be called the policeman State - would be the State of the future, lie expressed an exaggerated fear of the Irian in Lotions, and failed either to seem say that the State could be differentiated into a political State, and an administrative State, the former the organ of (iovernuient, aml the latter having a large degree of autonomy for dealing with the t rgankation of labour. , Mr. Chesterton postulated a Sate that was an entity, , with no division c,r differentiation, and against which ' there could le no appeal, as there would be none to whom an appeal could be made. But, the State of the future will dill, from what it is to-day. The func- tions of the State are continual), changing, and its di&es are becoming more vatted and extensive, its interference in 'wend relations is increasing. and the extenaiun of its functions will change its character, until it will become what Socialism means it

to become, "the organ of the collective. will:. he necennary condition of the advantage of collective ownership in that the Government should belong to everybody, and as Jean Jaures has said "Vt hen everybody is an official there will be no more officials." Mr. Chesterton eaid there could be no liberty without authority, but failed to say when authority would to overrun things as to become a slavery. Another prodigious error—startling, when we remember the intimacy of the speaker with Socialism arid Socialists—that be made was to 114111118 that under Socialism there would be no private property. Hisanalogy of the soldier's rifle, bearing • Govern- ment stamp. was altogether erroneous. Socialism does not imply a State where the individual could not own personal property, with which, if hecared, to use Mr. Chesterton's words " he could monkey." There is nothing incompatible between Socialist doctrine and private property. The aim of Socialism is simply to eliminate private property in capitol. Mr. Chesterton's fantastic prospect of a Socialist state pooling everything, of the man in buttons dealing out wages to men in chains, is too grotesque to he taken seriously. For, in one of his books he sass " Socialism is, as everyone knows, the proposal that all property should be nationally owned that it nray be more decently distributed." Property if distri- buted must become absolute possession, and in this lies the safeguard against the encroachment our individual liberty which he fancifully predicted. Men, to-day, find the guarantee of their liberty in their property. The only tneans of securing liberty for all is to give properly to all, and the only means of giving property to all is to socialite the instruments of production and distribu- tion. Why should men be less free when they travel on State railways, use municipal water. consume municipal gas, ride in publicly owned tram., eat municipal bread and drink municipal milk. than when they use these things when owned by private companiea ?

conzlusion. I would, in order to nugget the untrisdom of projecting into the Socialistic State the conditions that exist to-day, quote a Socialist writer, who says that " in order to ttansform. in the interests of all, capitalist property into social property the proletariat will have to show such a I.ower of organi,a- tion.to realise such progress,morally and intellectually. that it becomes absurd to suppose that generations trained in such a school would for a single moment siamit to any restraints whatever upon their full and complete liberty." -

. - R. PIERCE WILLIAMS. Warria§ton, Feb. 16th, 1911.

1 IRISH AFFAIRS. NOTES FOR IRISH READERS.

THE EVENT OF TIIE WEEK.

The del ate or, the address in Put linment gave the Unionists their opportunity of introducing a debate on Home Rule. That debate is tha event of illr week kr Irishmen and is not without very real significance to everybody in Great Britain who in concerned in the affairs of these islands. The bankrupt condition of the Opposition is seen in the singularly chequered history of the amendment itself. After revision and repeated reconstruction it was at length moved on Wednesday last by Mr. lan Malcolm and rieconded by Lord Hugh Carib Mr. %Intim should by this date have learned what value thinking men set upon his stale dish of anti-Irish oratior.s. This atol nothing else was the secret of his defeat when he contested North Salford in 1910. Baton Wednesday last he ploughed on through a morass of vial-violate data bearing no relation whatever to he Ireland of todav nor the cause of that emintry's nelf-government. the very ineptitude of the Unionist opening set the current cf feeling in the blouse strongly in favour of the 4:ooernment and Dome Pule. The curious line of Lord Hugh Cecil's argtiment—Sccratically and academically inquinilive as Mr. Asquith, to the great amusement of the House, christened it— was quite in keeping with the strange positron Lord Hugh has mule for himself in his party and in the William of the country. From an affected intellectual height tins superior scion of a superior stock dropped questions which an amatenr demoting ciety would have laughed to scorn. But we felt inclined to rub our eyes ~nd read again that part of his speech in which he nought to dispute the title of Ireland's nationality. That a man should utter such words seemed to us perfectly bewildering. there we are in the twentieth century. in the era or education and enlightened thinking, in the period famous for nothing so lunch as the ltp clear light which investigation and research hare poured on the records of defeat, and have pieced together the tangled and untoward doings of the in lace into a story of continuous and definite national tradition which in its variety of vieinsitudea and difficulties is perhaps unexampled in European history. It would probably astonish the astnte Lord, whose sincerity we never doubted, to learn that the Irish of to-day are less angered by the memories of the bloodnhedding of the Elizabethan and Cromwellian butchers than by the callous criticism which seeks to deprive as of our fired and most vital principle. The troopers at any rate gave us credit for being men, with hearts in our bodies which we were ready to sacrifice for the ideals of our minds. After Mr. Walter Long, whose Unionism has Dever been quite balanced since he ran away from his Irish constimency. came Sir Edward Carson. As everyone knows S;r Edward Carson is an advocate in a commanding place in his profession. Ile should therefore have the credit for understanding whatever words he used. And in the House of Commons where men meet to deliberate coolly and dispasnionatelv on the highest and smallest affairs, Sir Edward Carson. leader of the Irish Unionists, tells all whom it may concern that on this paramount question t.l Home Pule, a State matter demanding ram, al investigation, he is latter. That is his own description of himself, bin own adjective, sad we cannot help feeling that if the ancient and venerable House of Comrnonn in a free and deliberative nswenibly, and not a rraze-fighting ring, then Sir Edward Carson has no right to be there when this subject, which he candidly tells us he loathes, is debated. THE PREMIER AND TRUE IMPERIALISM.

After the poor and ineffective display rn the :intis Home Rule side, the debate gives one three strong impressions. The hirer was the distinctly dear and firm position taken up by the Prime Minister. Nothing could be more significant of the real attitude of the strf ng man of the Cabinet Innards Home Rule then the way in which he accepted Lord Hugh Cecil's challenge to consult his (Mr. Asquith's) past speeches on the subject. Terse, telling sentences uttered now eighteen years. now at the tarpons Albert Hall meeting, now at Hull and at Fife, were read out Lry the Premier to show that far from the obscurity alleged in the amendment, there had been all along an unshaken adherence on his part to this sheer anchor of real liberalism. Experience he said, hail demonstrated the incapacity of the Parliament at Westminster to cope with Irish affairs, and in wools which we feel most beconie classic for their beauty and wisdom, Mr. Asquith concluded We look forward to see French and English, Boer and Briton, Celt and Saxon, each bringing his own tributary mingling their con- fluent waters in the stream of Imperial Unity—use Throne, one Empire, one people, diverse in origin and lin race, but all alike charged with the liberty and renponeibility of self-government in their own local affairs. That surely is the goal of a really true Imperialist, and it into that goal thnt rue steps are set." Mr. Malcolm and Lord Hugh Cecil deserve the undying gratitude of all progresvive thinkers for having occasioned so eloquent and 8., pregnant a wane as that inspiring peroration of the Prender'a.

The Wart ington Musical Sor iety's second sulnicrip- lion concoct in the Parr Hall on wedneeday evening presented many r,ints of interest and drew glide a representative, if cot a very numerous, audience. Granted a capable principal -and the concert clearly demonstrated Mies Lucy Pierce ti be stich— the difficulties of Teelmikowaky s B fiat minor concerto are chiefly those of the orchestra, inasmuch as the score abounds in pitfalls for the unwary instrumentalist in its nunterons and very onerous entries for the various sections of ae orchestra. And, curiously. such points of weakness as were noticeable on Wednesday were those of intonation rather than if precision. In this connection it can, of comae, hardly Ice expected that a body of players, constiteted I the scriety's orchestra, can pair them- selves off in any ideal mariner with the soloist. But while there were some excusable olocuritiee of execution it is leas easy of explanatima that faulty intonation should extend from the strings into the worst-wind. Interest in the way in which our local players acquit themselves, sugges's this precedence in the order of our remarks, and notwithstanding what has been said, it must be admitted that the musical society's orchestra did marvellously well in coetributing to the elicit. of Tschaikowsky's great work. Of Mies Pierces playing little can be said which is not high praise. Gifted with a technique embracing a command of the instrument of the most finished and mature order, her style and temperament incline distinctly towards the robust and the vivacious. In the concerto Miss Pierre had therefore congenial material to Work upon, the Russian composer's wealth of melody - often sin kind 'startlingly new to English eats--and his often massive and vividly picturesque harmonies being presented with the most convincing earnestness and rigour. Needless to say Miss Pierce was vociferously applauded on the conclusion of no strenuous a work and one involving no mean feat of inernoty. Later in the evening Mioo Pierce played Schumann's Romance in I" sharp major. •nd the better known " La rattipanella " ot Paganini-Liszt most attistically, rempondieg to an enthusiastic encore with a rendering of a gavotte by Gluck quite charming in its glaceftil The oichestra dealt with Beethoven's fifth symphony with manifestly greater comfort and ease than the concerto, and this glorirlis work ran its course smoothly and without serious hitebalheit it threatened to become irk.orne by reason of its inordinate length. Of the several movement's the " andante " was probe& y the best in an allrorind sense. For although the symphony pre/septa serious executant difficulties, they seem to be of a more manageable kind than those of the concerto, the steady stream of Beethoven's delicious combination of melo ly and harmony in the slow movement carrying one in a langnorous mood over all small obstacles. The overture to "Per Friesehutz " and Jarnefelt's most engaging "Prieludium" complete the enumeration of the orrhestrsl it •ms. in regard to the performsree of which the Mueical Society's mstrumental meml era and Mr. Cron]. y its hard-working conductor are to he warmly congratulated. hoc!bra Arnell sang "Ombra mai fu " from Handel's " Rinaldo" in a rich contralto voice and with rreat depth of feeling. The society's vocal members contributed three numbers during the evening.
